When value cache is quantized per-channel, the\naccuracy significantly worsens regardless of how key cache\nis quantized.\nOB 3. When using a lower numerical precision such as\nINT2, the most accurate approach is to quantize key cache\nper-channel and value cache per-token.\n3.2. Why Key and Value Cache Should Quantize Along\nDifferent Dimensions?\nIn Table 1, we observe that quantizing key cache per-channel\nand value cache per-token to 2bit results in a very small accu-\nracy drop. Here we analyze why this configuration delivers\nbetter accuracy. In Figure 2 we visualize the original KV\ncache distribution at different layers. We observe that in\nkey cache, some fixed channels exhibit very large mag-\nnitudes, whereas in value cache, there is no significant\npattern for outliers.\nAnalysis of Key Cache.\nThe above observation for key\ncache aligns with previous findings that certain fixed\ncolumns in activations exhibit larger outliers (Dettmers et al.,\n2022; Lin et al., 2023). The persistence of outliers within\neach channel means that per-channel quantization can con-\nfine the quantization error to each individual channel, with-\nout impacting the other normal channels. Thus, Figure 2\nexplains why key cache should be quantized per-channel.\nIn Table 2 we show key cache relative reconstruction error\n∥XK−X′\nK\nXK\n∥F , along with the relative attention score error\n∥A−A′\nA\n∥F where A′ = Softmax(tQX\n′⊤\nK ). We observe that\nthe per-token quantization can lead to almost 5× larger at-\ntention score error than per-channel quantization, which is\nconsistent with Figure 2.\nTable 2: The relative error statistics averaged over all layers\nand all heads\nLlama-2-13B\nK Per-Token\nK Per-Channel\nAvg. ∥\nXK−X′\nK\nXK\n∥F\n13.67\n4.55\nAvg. ∥A−A′\nA\n∥F\n47.0\n9.6\nAttention sparsity\n84.3%\nV Per-Token\nV Per-Channel\nAvg. ∥\nXV −X′\nV\nXV\n∥F\n4.57\n3.73\nAvg. ∆\n3.55\n49.89\nAnalysis of Value Cache.\nUnlike key cache, value cache\ndoes not show the channel-wise outlier pattern. Furthermore,\n4\n\n\n=== PDF page 5 ===\nKIVI: A Tuning-Free Asymmetric 2bit Quantization for KV Cache\nQ_MatMul\nConcat\nQuantization by Channel\nQuantization by Token\nMatMul\nPrefill Phase\nDecoding Phase*\nKVCache\n* we omit the value cache and\n attention output calculation\nFull Precision Tensor\nLow Precision Tensor\nFigure 3: The overview of KIVIalgorithm. For ease of illustration, we omit the value cache and attention output parts. The\ndetailed pseudo-code is provided in Algorithm 1. Here “Q_Matmul” is the mix-precision matrix multiplication which fuses\nthe dequantization with matrix multiplication at the tiling level.\nFigure 2 alone cannot explain OB2, which indicates value\ncache should only be quantized per-token. This is because\nFigure 2 implies that errors should be comparable for both\nper-token and per-channel quantization, given the absence\nof a clear pattern. As shown in Equation (1), value cache\nis used to calculate the attention output tO. Instead of\nanalyzing the quantization error of value cache XV , in\nTable 2 we analyze the relative error ∆= ∥AXV −AX′\nV\nAXV\n∥F\nwith different quantization configurations. Surprisingly, we\nobserve that the per-token quantization error is almost 15×\nsmaller than per-channel quantization, which explains why\nOB2 happens. The intuition behind this observation stems\nfrom the attention sparsity. Equation (1) can be written as:\n[AXV ]i∗=\nlprompt\nX\nj=1\nAij[XV ]j∗,\n(2)\nwhere [XV ]j∗is the j-th row of XV . From Equation (2),\nthe attention output is the weighted summation of value\ncache across various tokens, with the weights being the\nattention scores. Since the attention score is highly sparse\n(Tian et al., 2023), the output is just the combination of\nvalue caches of a few important tokens. The per-token\nquantization can confine the error to each individual token.\nThus, quantizing other tokens does not affect the accuracy\nof important tokens. Thus, per-token quantization leads to a\nmuch smaller relative error ∆.\n3.3. KIVI: Algorithm and System Support\nAlgorithm.\nAs we previously analyzed, key cache should\nbe quantized per-channel and value cache should be quan-\ntized per-token. Recall that key and value cache of newly\ngenerated tokens arrive sequentially. From the implemen-\ntation perspective, per-token quantization aligns well with\nstreaming settings, allowing newly quantized tensors to be\ndirectly appended to the existing quantized value cache\nby token dimension. However, for per-channel quantiza-\ntion, the quantization process spans across different tokens,\nwhich cannot be directly implemented in the streaming set-\nting. As shown in Figure 3, our key idea to solve this prob-\nlem is to group key cache every G tokens and quantize them\nseparately. Because the number of tokens in XK can be ar-\nbitrary, we split XK into two parts, namely, the grouped part\nXKg = XK[: l −r] and residual part XKr = XK[l −r :],\nwhere l is the number of tokens inside the current key cache\nXK, r is the number of residual tokens, where l −r can be\ndivisible by G.\nSince XKg can be evenly divided into (l −r)/G groups,\nwe only store Q(XKg) with group-wise quantization, while\nXKr is kept in full precision. During the decoding process,\neach newly arrived key cache tK is added to XKr and once\nXKr reaches R tokens, which is a hyperparameter - residual\nlength, we quantize and concatenate it with the previously\nquantized Q(XKG). Then we reset XKr to an empty tensor.\nWe note that R should be divisible by G. With tiled matrix\nmultiplication, the raw attention logits is then calculated as:\nAg = tQQ(X⊤\nKg),\nXKr = Concat([XKr, tK]),\nAr = tQX⊤\nKr,\nA = Concat([Ag, Ar]).\n(3)\nFor value cache, similar to key cache, we also split it into\ntwo parts and keep the most recent value cache in full pre-\ncision, namely, XVg and XVr. Specifically, we maintain a\nqueue and each newly arrived value cache is pushed into\nthe queue. Once the queue reaches the predefined residual\nlength R, the most outdated value cache is poped. Then the\npoped value cache is quantized per-token and concatenated\nwith the previously quantized value cache along the token\ndimension.\nAs shown in Figure 3, we also emphasize that during the\nprefill phase, the exact key and value tensors are passed to\nthe next layers, although only the quantized KV cache is\nretained in memory. The whole algorithm can be found in\nAppendix A Algorithm 1.\n5\n\n\n=== PDF page 6 ===\nKIVI: A Tuning-Free Asymmetric 2bit Quantization for KV Cache\nAnalysis.\nIn KIVI, the grouped key cache XKg and value\ncache XVg is quantized, while the residual key cache XKr\nand value cache XVr is kept in full precision. By design,\nthere are at most R tokens inside XKr or XVr. In practice,\nwe set R ≤128 and the sequence length lprompt + lgen is\noften much longer than R. Thus the memory overhead from\nXKr and XVr is negligible when considering the benefit\nfrom extreme low-bit quantization, especially for the long\ncontext scenarios. Also, since the newly arrived key and\nvalue tensors are added to XKr and XVr in full precision,\nKIVImaintains a full precision KV cache sliding window\nfor the local relevant tokens. This window size is expected\nto be R\n2 for key cache, and R for value cache. Later in the\nexperiment section, we show that this full precision sliding\nwindow is crucial for obtaining desirable performance on\nhard tasks, such as GSM8K.\nSystem Support.\nWe provide a hardware-friendly imple-\nmentation for running KIVI on GPUs. To minimize the\noverhead, we have fused the dequantization process with\nmatrix multiplication, e.g., Q_MatMul in Figure 3, using\nCUDA. We also implement the group-wise quantization ker-\nnel in Triton. Our method is fully compatible with weight-\nonly quantization.\n4. Experiments\n4.1. Settings\nModels.\nWe evaluate KIVI using two popular recent\nmodel families: Llama/Llama-2 (Touvron et al., 2023a;b)),\nFalcon (Penedo et al., 2023) and Mistral (Jiang et al., 2023).\nWe note that the Llama and Mistral model is based on multi-\nhead attention, while Falcon is based on multi-query atten-\ntion (Shazeer, 2019). We use the Hugging Face Transform-\ners codebase and implement the KIVIalgorithm based on\nit. Following previous work (Sheng et al., 2023), the group\nsize G in Algorithm 1 for quantization is set as 32 across all\nexperiments, the residual length R for key and value cache\nis set to 128.\nTasks.\nAs we analyzed in Section 2, the KV cache size\ngrows larger with longer context. Thus we evaluate KIVI\nunder the normal context length and long context setting,\nrespectively. Specifically, we adopt generation tasks from\nLM-Eval (Gao et al., 2021) for normal context length eval-\nuation and LongBench (Bai et al., 2023) for long context\nevaluation, respectively1. For LM-eval, we adopt CoQA\n(Exact match accuracy), TruthfulQA (BLEU score), and\nGSM8K (Exact match accuracy). For LongBench, we chose\n1The closed-end tasks such as MMLU are not ideal to evaluate\nKIVIsince they only involve one decoding step and directly fetch\nthe output logits, which is not suitable for studying the impact of\ncompressed KV cache.\ntasks from four subgroups. Specifically, Qasper (F1 score)\nis a Single-Document QA task; QMSum (ROUGE score)\nand MultiNews (ROUGE score) are Summarization tasks;\nTREC (classification score), TriviaQA (F1 score), and SAM-\nSum (ROUGE score) are Few-shot Learning tasks; and LCC\n(similarity score) and RepoBench-P (similarity score) is\nCode Completion task. The maximum sequence length in\nLongBench was set to 8192 for the Mistral model, and 4096\nfor other models.\n4.2. Accuracy and Efficiency Analysis\n4.2.1. COMPARISON BETWEEN DIFFERENT\nQUANTIZATION CONFIGURATIONS\nWe first utilize the fake quantization to demonstrate the\neffectiveness of our asymmetric quantization, namely, quan-\ntizing key cache per-channel and value cache per-token.\nHere fake quantization is exactly the same as in Table 1.\nThe results are shown in Table 3. We observe that “2bit (K\nper-channel, V per-token)” consistently achieves the best re-\nsults compared to all other configurations. This is consistent\nwith our previous analysis. We also note that for hard gener-\nation tasks such as GSM8K, the fake “2bit (K per-channel,\nV per-token)” quantization results are significantly worse\nthan the full precision counterparts. However for KIVIin\nTable 3, we observe accuracy drop is only around 2% for\nGSM8K across different models. As we analyzed in Section\n3.3, the difference between fake “2bit (K per-channel, V\nper-token)” quantization and KIVIis that KIVImaintains\na full precision key and value cache sliding window for\nthe local relevant tokens. This sliding window is crucial\nto maintaining accuracy for hard generation tasks such as\nmathematical reasoning.\n4.2.2. ACCURACY COMPARISON ON GENERATION\nTASKS\nLM-Eval Results.\nWe benchmark KIVIon CoQA, Truth-\nfulQA, and GSM8K tasks using LM-Eval framework. All\ndataset parameters were set to default. We compare the\nstandard 16bit configuration with our KIVI compression\ntechniques across Llama-2-7B, Llama-2-13B, Falcon-7B,\nand Mistral-7B. As shown in Table 3, we observe that for\nthe Llama and Mistral model, KIVIonly has up to 2% ac-\ncuracy drop despite the KV cache being stored in 2bit. For\ninstance, in the Llama-2-7B model, the transition from 16bit\nto 2bit only slightly decreases accuracy. Similar trends are\nobserved in other Llama-family models. For Falcon-7B,\nsince it adopts multi-query attention and only has one head\nfor KV cache, it is already highly compressed compared to\nLlama-based models. Thus in Table 3, 4bit KIVIis needed\nto maintain the accuracy, while 2bit KIVImay have a large\naccuracy drop in this case.\n6\n\n\n=== PDF page 7 ===\nKIVI: A Tuning-Free Asymmetric 2bit Quantization for KV Cache\nTable 3: Performance comparison between 16bit, four fake\n2bit KV cache quantization similar to those in Table 1,\nKIVI-2 (2bit) / KIVI-4 (4bit) across various models. ABLATION\nIn this section, we benchmark KIVI on GSM8K, one of\nthe hardest generation tasks, to show the effect of hyperpa-\nrameters group size G and residual length R on the model\nperformance. For full results of KIVIwith a residual length\nof 32, please refer to Appendix B.\nThe effect of group size. We fix the residual length at 128\nand vary the group sizes to 32, 64, and 128. From Table 5,\nwe observe that group sizes 32 and 64 yield similar results,\nwhereas the performance significantly decreases when the\ngroup size reaches 128.\nFigure 4: Memory usage and throughput comparison be-\ntween 2bit KIVI and 16bit baseline. KIVI can achieve\nhigher throughput by enabling a larger batch size.\nThe effect of residual length. We fix the group size at 32\nand vary the residual length across 32, 64, 96, and 128. As\nshown in Table 5, there is no consistent pattern between\nresidual lengths and model accuracy. Namely, while a resid-\nual length of 128 achieves good results, 32 and 96 yield\nsimilar outcomes, but a residual length of 64 results in the\nworst performance.\n4.2.4. EFFICIENCY COMPARISON\nTo evaluate the wall-clock time efficiency of KIVI, follow-\ning vLLM (Kwon et al., 2023a), we synthesize workloads\nbased on ShareGPT (sha, 2023), which contain input and\noutput texts of real LLM services. In average, the dataset\nfeatures an input prompt length lprompt of 161 and an output\nlength lgen of 338 (Kwon et al., 2023a). We increase the\nbatch size until out of memory and report the peak memory\nusage and throughput between KIVI(with residual length\n32 and 128) and FP16 baseline for the Llama-2-7B model.\nThe hardware here is a single NVIDIA A100 GPU (80GB).\nAs shown in Figure 4, with similar maximum memory us-\nage, KIVI enables up to 4× larger batch size and gives\n2.35× ∼3.47× larger throughput. This throughput num-\nber can grow larger with longer context length and output\nlength. We also note that this speedup can be greatly in-\ncreased if we further fuse the KV cache quantization\nprocess with previous operations. We leave it as one of\nthe future work.\n7\n\n\n=== PDF page 8 ===\nKIVI: A Tuning-Free Asymmetric 2bit Quantization for KV Cache\nTable 4: Performance evaluation of KIVIon various models across a range of benchmarks in LongBench. Related Work\nQuantization techniques have been widely applied in Large\nLanguage Models (Frantar et al., 2022; Lin et al., 2023;\nKim et al., 2023; Xu et al., 2023). A main branch of LLM\nquantization is weight-only quantization, which involves\nthe quantization of model weights to lower precision. For\ninstance, AWQ (Lin et al., 2023) cleverly quantizes model\nweights to INT4 and INT3 using an activation-aware manner.\nGPTQ (Frantar et al., 2022) utilizes approximate second-\norder information to quantize model weights both accurately\nand efficiently. SqueezeLLM (Kim et al., 2023) adopts the\nconcept of sensitivity-based non-uniform quantization along\nwith Dense-and-Sparse decomposition. This line of work is\northogonal to ours, as they can be combined together.\nSmoothQuant (Xiao et al., 2023a) is a post-training quan-\ntization method that is more closely related to our work.\nThis method uses equivalent transformations to balance\nthe quantization complexity for both activation and weight,\nmaking the activation easier to quantize. SmoothQuant can\ncompress KV cache to 8bit with minor performance loss.\nHowever, it faces a significant accuracy drop when scaled\ndown to 4bit or less.\nvLLM (Kwon et al., 2023b) and S3 (Jin et al., 2023) are\nsystem-level works, which include memory management\nthrough the use of PagedAttention or memory usage predic-\ntion. These approaches can lower the memory requirements\nof KV cache and simultaneously increase model throughput.\nThis research direction is orthogonal to our work, as such\nsystem-level optimizations can also be applied to enhance\nour algorithm.\nSeveral other works also consider compressing KV cache\nby evicting unimportant tokens. H2O (Zhang et al., 2023)\nretains only a small portion of tokens that contribute signifi-\ncantly to the attention scores. StreamingLLM (Xiao et al.,\n2023b) is based on the observation of “attention sink” and\nmaintains only a few initial tokens to preserve performance.\nUnlike these works, our KIVIretains all input tokens and\ncompresses them into lower precision. This line of work is\northogonal to ours, as they also can be combined together.\n6. Conclusion and Future Work\nIn this paper, we systematically analyze KV cache element\ndistribution in popular LLMs. We conclude that key cache\nshould be quantized per-channel and value cache should\nbe quantized per token. Based on these observations, we\npropose KIVI, a plug-and-play 2bit KV cache quantization\nalgorithm without the need for any tuning. In real LLM\nworkload, KIVI allows up to 4× larger batch sizes and\n3.47× throughput. In the future, we will further optimize\nthe implementation to reduce the overhead of quantization\nprocess during the prefill and decoding phase.\n8\n\n\n=== PDF page 9 ===\nKIVI: A Tuning-Free Asymmetric 2bit Quantization for KV Cache\n7. Detailed Implementations\nIn this section, we present the algorithm for KIVIas dis-\ncussed in Section 3.3. Specifically, we provide the pseu-\ndocode for KIVIwhen calculating the attention output in\nthe prefill and decoding phases.\nAlgorithm 1: The KIVIPrefill & Decoding Algorithm\nparameter: group size G, residual length R\nprocedure Prefill:\nInput: X ∈Rlprompt×d\nXK = XWK, XV = XWV\nXVg = XV [: lprompt−R], XVr = XV [lprompt−R :]\nQ(XVg) ←\nGroupQuant(XVg, dim=token, numGroup=d//G)\nQ(XKg), XKr ←KeyQuant(XK)\nKV cache ←Q(XKg), XKr, Q(XVg), XVr\nreturn XK, XV\nend\nprocedure Decoding:\nInput: KV cache, t ∈R1×d\ntQ = tWQ, tK = tWK, tV = tWV\nQ(XKg), XKr, Q(XVg), XVr ←KV cache\nXKr ←Concat([XKr, tK], dim=token)\nXVr ←Concat([XVr, tV ], dim=token)\nif len(XKr) = R then\nQ(XKr), _ ←KeyQuant(XKr)\nQ(XKg) ←\nConcat([Q(XKg), Q(XKr)], dim=token)\nXKr ←empty tensor.\nend\nif len(XVr) > R then\nQ(XV ′r ) ←GroupQuant(XVr[:\n−R], dim=token, numGroup = d//G)\nQ(XVg) ←\nConcat([Q(XVg), Q(XV ′r )], dim=token)\nXVr ←XVr[−R :]\nend\nA ←Concat([tQQ(XKg)⊤, tQX⊤\nKr], dim=token)\nAg = Softmax(A)[: −R], Ar =\nSoftmax(A)[−R :]\ntO ←AgQ(XVg) + ArXVr\nKV cache ←Q(XKg), XKr, Q(XVg), XVr\nreturn tO\nend\nfunction KeyQuant(XK ∈Rl×d):\nr = l%R,\nXKg = XK[: l −r], XKr = XK[l −r :]\nQ(XKg) ←\nGroupQuant(XKg, dim=channel, numGroup=l//G)\nreturn Q(XKg), XKr\nend\nB. More Experimental Results\nIn our efficiency evaluation, we observe that with a residual\nlength of 32, KIVIachieves a significantly higher memory\ncompression rate, which in turn leads to increased through-\nput. Additionally, our ablation study reveals that changing\nthe residual length from 128 to 32 does not result in a sub-\nstantial performance gap. We demonstrate KIVI with a\nresidual length of 32 across all benchmark datasets. "=== PDF page 1 ===\nH2O: Heavy-Hitter Oracle for Efficient Generative Inference of\nLarge Language Models\nZhenyu Zhang1, Ying Sheng2, Tianyi Zhou3, Tianlong Chen1, Lianmin Zheng4, Ruisi Cai1,\nZhao Song5, Yuandong Tian6, Christopher Ré2, Clark Barrett2, Zhangyang Wang1, Beidi Chen6,7\n1University of Texas at Austin, 2Stanford University, 3University of California, San Diego,\n4University of California, Berkeley, 5Adobe Research, 6Meta AI (FAIR), 7Carnegie Mellon University\n{zhenyu.zhang,tianlong.chen,ruisi.cai,atlaswang}@utexas.edu, ying1123@stanford.edu,\n{chrismre,barrett}@cs.stanford.edu, t8zhou@ucsd.edu, lianminzheng@gmail.com,\nzsong@adobe.com, yuandong@meta.com, beidic@andrew.cmu.edu\nJune 27, 2023\nAbstract\nLarge Language Models (LLMs), despite their recent impressive accomplishments, are notably cost-\nprohibitive to deploy, particularly for applications involving long-content generation, such as dialogue\nsystems and story writing. Often, a large amount of transient state information, referred to as the KV\ncache, is stored in GPU memory in addition to model parameters, scaling linearly with the sequence\nlength and batch size. In this paper, we introduce a novel approach for implementing the KV cache which\nsignificantly reduces its memory footprint. Our approach is based on the noteworthy observation that a\nsmall portion of tokens contributes most of the value when computing attention scores. We call these\ntokens Heavy Hitters (H2). Through a comprehensive investigation, we find that (i) the emergence of H2\nis natural and strongly correlates with the frequent co-occurrence of tokens in the text, and (ii) removing\nthem results in significant performance degradation. Based on these insights, we propose Heavy Hitter\nOracle (H2O), a KV cache eviction policy that dynamically retains a balance of recent and H2 tokens. We\nformulate the KV cache eviction as a dynamic submodular problem and prove (under mild assumptions)\na theoretical guarantee for our novel eviction algorithm which could help guide future work. We validate\nthe accuracy of our algorithm with OPT, LLaMA, and GPT-NeoX across a wide range of tasks. Our\nimplementation of H2O with 20% heavy hitters improves the throughput over three leading inference\nsystems DeepSpeed Zero-Inference, Hugging Face Accelerate, and FlexGen by up to 29×, 29×, and 3×\non OPT-6.7B and OPT-30B. With the same batch size, H2O can reduce the latency by up to 1.9×. The\ncode is available at https://github.com/FMInference/H2O.\n1\nIntroduction\nLarge Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable proficiency in a wide range of natural language\nprocessing applications such as content creation, summarization, and dialogue systems [1, 2, 3, 4]. However,\ntheir deployment is very costly. In addition to the widely-studied bottlenecks of model size and the quadratic\ncost of attention layers, the problem of the size of the KV cache, which stores the intermediate attention key\nand values during generation to avoid re-computation, is becoming increasingly prominent [5]. For instance,\na 30 billion-parameter model with an input batch size of 128 and a sequence length of 1024 results in 180GB\nof KV cache. A natural approach is to limit its maximum size as is done in classical software or hardware\ncaches [6]. However, it is challenging to reduce KV cache memory footprints in LLMs without accuracy drops.\nWhile there exists substantial literature on sparse attention approximation in training, they have not\nseen wide adoption for alleviating KV cache bottleneck. First, most existing methods, e.g., Reformer [7],\nFlash Attention [8], and Performer [9], are designed to overcome the quadratic memory required by attention\nmechanisms when modeling long sequences but still require a large cache size. Second, variants like sparse\ntransformers [10] or multi-query attention [11, 12, 5] can reduce the cache size, but directly applying them on\n1\narXiv:2306.14048v1  [cs.LG]  24 Jun 2023\n\n\n=== PDF page 2 ===\n0.1\n0.1\n0.5\nChildren laughed\nand\nin\nthe\nsunny\npark\n...\n.\nplayed\n0.2\n0.1\n1\n0.9\n0.6\n0.2\n0.1\n0.1\n0.6\n1.4\n0.6\n1.5\n0.5\n0.4\n0.1\n0.4\n0.1\nQuery\nKey\nValue\nStatic Sparsity (Strided)\nStatic Sparsity (Local)\nStatic Sparsity w. \nDynamic Sparsity\nFigure 1: Upper plots illustrate symbolic plots of an attention map deploying different KV cache policies in LLM\ngeneration. Lower right: contrasts their accuracy-memory trade-off. Left: the overview of H2O framework.\npre-trained LLMs for generation results in high miss rates and degrades the accuracy as shown in Figure 1.\nFinally, some recent advances such as gisting tokens [13] can learn to compress the KV cache for documents,\nbut their expensive eviction policies are difficult to deploy during generation.\nTherefore, an ideal KV cache should have (i) a small cache size to reduce memory footprint, (ii) a low\nmiss rate to maintain the performance and long-content generation ability of LLMs, and (iii) a low-cost\neviction policy to reduce the wall-clock time during generation. However, there are three technical challenges.\nFirst, it is not immediately clear whether the size of the KV cache can be restricted—each decoding step\nmight, in principle, require access to all previous attention keys and values. Second, identifying an optimal\neviction policy that maintains generation accuracy is a combinatorial problem1. Finally, even if an optimal\npolicy can be brute-forced, it is infeasible for deployment on real-world applications.\nFortunately, our preliminary exploration has yielded intriguing observations about the empirical properties\nof LLMs. These findings pave the way for the potential design of an efficient KV cache.\nSparsity for small cache size: We observe that even when trained densely, the attention matrices of LLMs\nare over 95% sparse at inference time (shown in Figure 2). This holds for a wide range of pre-trained LLMs.\nTherefore, only 5% of the KV cache is sufficient for decoding the same output token at each generation step,\nwhich suggests it may be possible to have up to a 20× reduction in KV cache size without an accuracy drop.\nHeavy-Hitters for low miss rate: We discover that the accumulated attention scores of all tokens in\nattention blocks adhere to a power-law distribution. It suggests that there exists a small set of influential\ntokens that are critical during generation, named heavy-hitters (H2). H2 provides an opportunity to step\naway from the combinatorial search problem and identify an eviction policy that maintains accuracy.\nGreedy algorithm for low-cost policy: we surprisingly find that retaining the H2 based on local statistics at\neach decoding step—summing the attention scores of only the preceding tokens—is as effective as considering\nthe attention of future tokens (shown in Figure 2).\nBased on the above, we first rigorously define the generative process of LLMs operating with a size-\nconstrained KV cache in Section 2.1. Then we propose Heavy-Hitter Oracle (H2O), a framework that exploits\nthe properties of LLMs and uses simple, low-cost eviction policies that retrain the quality of LLMs throughout\nthe generation process. Specifically,\n• In Section 3, we explore the emergence of H2 in attention, revealing their fundamental and critical roles:\n(i) H2 exhibit a strong correlation of frequently co-occurring words in textual data; and (ii) removing H2\ncompletely damages the model’s functionality. We demonstrate that H2 can largely lower the cache miss\nrate of the existing policies mentioned above. Theoretically, assuming the attention scheme is submodular,\nH2 corresponds to a greedy algorithm and is therefore near-optimal.\n• In Section 4, we present a greedy but low-cost variant of H2 which is dynamically determined by the\n1Belady’s Algorithm is optimal for standard cache, but not necessarily for KV cache.\n2\n\n\n=== PDF page 3 ===\naccumulated attention score at each decoding step. We formulate the eviction policy with greedy H2 as a\nvariant of dynamic submodular maximization. The analysis shows that it results in a similar generative\nprocess as the one using the H2 eviction policy.\nWe perform extensive experiments on OPT, LLaMA, and GPT-NeoX on a single NVIDIA A100 (80GB)\nGPU to evaluate H2O across a range of tasks from lm-eval-harness [14] and HELM [15]. We implement\nH2O on top of FlexGen that can easily adapt different cache eviction techniques to produce a system with\nhigh-throughput inference. Performance experiments show our framework achieves 29×, 29×, 3× higher\nthroughputs compared to three leading inference systems, DeepSpeed Zero-Inference [16], Hugging Face\nAccelerate [17], and FlexGen [18] respectively. With the same batch size, H2O achieves up to 1.9× lower\nlatency compare to FlexGen.\n2\nRelated Work and Problem Setting\nQuantization, Pruning, Distillation for Inference.\nTo mitigate the resource cost of model inference,\nvarious model compression algorithms have been investigated for decades. This big family roughly lies\ninto three groups: (1) quantization [19, 20, 21, 22]; (2) pruning or sparsity [23, 24, 25, 26]; (3) and\ndistillation [27, 28, 29, 30]. They pursue efficient inference from orthogonal perspectives and can also be\norganically integrated. The techniques studied in this paper are closely related to sparsity but focus on a\ndifferent inference bottleneck, KV cache.\nSparse, Low-rank Attention Approx.\nThe quadratic computational complexity of attention modules\nis one of the major bottlenecks of transformer inference [31]. Various efforts are devoted to addressing this\nchallenge like Reformer [7], Performer [9], Sparse Transformer [10], etc. The most relevant works that design\nalgorithms to reduce KV cache memory footprint, are considered as our baselines in this paper.\nCaching.\nCaching, which plays a pivotal role in optimizing system performance, entails the development of\neffective eviction policies to handle frequently accessed data. Conventional approaches such as Least Recently\nUsed and Least Frequently Used [32, 33] prioritize the recency and frequency of data access. And the design\nof KV cache encounters many similar challenges as traditional caching.\nLLM Inference Breakdown.\nThe generative procedure of LLMs encompasses two distinct phases: (i) the\nprompt phase, in which an input sequence is utilized to produce the KV cache (consisting of the key and\nvalue embeddings), similar to the forward pass employed during LLM training; and (ii) the token generation\nphase, which leverages and updates the KV cache to generate new tokens incrementally. Each generation step\nrelies on the previously generated tokens. The primary focus of this paper is to enhance the efficiency of the\nKV cache in attention during the token generation phase, thereby accelerating LLM inference.\n2.1\nProblem Formulation\nWe formally define the generative process with limited KV cache size. Denote attention query matrix as\nQ ∈Rn×d and key matrix as K ∈Rn×d. Qi,∗represents the i-th row of Q and K≤i,∗represents the first i\nrows of K. Let k denote the budget of space and k < n. For simplicity, KSi,∗denotes a sub-matrix of K\nwhich selects Si rows from K. Eviction policy is defined as:\nDefinition 2.1 (Eviction Policy, informal). Let Si−1 denote the source set. Let Si denote the target set. We\ndefined the eviction policy g : Si−1 →Si such that\n• |Si| = k (KV cache size is not changing over the time)\n• |Si\\Si−1| ≤1 or equivalently |Si ∩Si−1| ≥k −1 (we can evict at most 1 KV in the KV cache)\nThen, we define the generative process with our eviction policy.\nDefinition 2.2 (The generative process with eviction policy, informal). Let k denote the size of the KV\ncache. For each i ∈[n], for the i-th token, we have\n• Let Si ⊂[n] denote the tokens in KV cache when predicting the i-th token.\n• The information we have is a length-i vector oi := D−1\ni\n· exp(Qi,∗(KSi,∗)⊤) (normalized attention)\n3\n\n\n=== PDF page 4 ===\n(a)\n(b)\n(c)\n(d)\nCOPA\nMathQA\nOpenBookQA\nPiQA\nRTE\nWinogrande\nFigure 2: (a) Attention Sparsity in pre-trained OPT models. (b) The distribution of accumulated attention scores\nwith respect to the corresponding word (red scatter) and the co-occurrence times of words in the data (gray curve).\nThe x-axis represents the word index in the vocabulary. (c) The performance comparison between the baseline model\nwith full KV and the model w.o. heavy hitter. Our goal is to find a KV cache eviction policy such that the output of the generative process is\nsimilar or comparable to the original one without limiting the cache size.\n3\nObservations\nWe present two key empirical insights of LLMs that inspire the design of H2O, as follows.\n3.1\nSparsity for Small Cache Size\nWe first show an observation on the sparsity of attention in pre-trained LLMs. Then we discuss how it can\npotentially unlock the possibility of reducing KV cache size without an accuracy drop. Given the normalized\nattention score Softmax(QK⊤) matrix that is calculated by the query matrix Q and the key matrix K, we\nset the threshold as one percent of the maximum value in each row and calculates the corresponding sparsity.\nObservation.\nWe conduct zero-shot inference with the pre-trained OPT model on the validation set of\nWiki-Text-103. We plot the layer-wise sparsity within attention blocks and visualize the normalized attention\nscore matrix. The results are presented in Figure 2 (a). We observe that although the LLMs are densely\ntrained, the resulting attention score matrices are highly sparse, with a sparsity over 95% in almost all layers.\nInsights.\nThe attention blocks’ sparsity suggests that access to all previous key and value embeddings is\nunnecessary for generating the next token. This suggests it is possible to evict unessential KV embeddings\nand reduce the requirement of KV cache during generation.\n3.2\nHeavy-Hitters for Low Miss Rate\nThe previous section showed the sparse nature of attention blocks in pre-trained LLMs, which provides the\nopportunity for designing small KV cache size while still maintaining the performance of LLMs. However,\ndetermining the best eviction policy that preserves generation accuracy presents a combinatorial challenge.\nAlthough Belady’s Algorithm [34] is optimal and easy to compute for standard cache (offline), it is not\napplicable for KV cache design. Because once evicting important KVs, it could destroy the performance of\nLLMs due to the sequential dependency of LLM generation.\nObservation.\nFortunately, in the early stage of our exploration, we find that the accumulated attention\nscores of all the tokens within attention blocks follow a power-law distribution, as shown in Figure 2. This\nsuggests the existence of a small set of tokens that are critical during generation. We denote those tokens\nas heavy-hitters (H2). In order to verify the importance of these tokens, we compare the quality of LLM\n4\n\n\n=== PDF page 5 ===\ngeneration after masking heavy hitters with that of the original model. Not surprisingly, as shown in Figure 2,\nthe accuracy drops drastically, confirming the importance of those tokens. Additionally, we can see the\naccumulated attention score of each word (in red dots) have a high correlation with their co-occurrences in\nthe data (gray curve).\nAnalysis.\nFirst, based on H2, we see an opportunity to side-step from the combinatorial search problem\nand design a KV cache eviction policy that preserves the LLM generation quality. We conduct an empirical\nstudy implementing a KV cache eviction policy that retains only the H2 and the recent KV embeddings in\nthe cache. The intuition is that recent words typically exhibit stronger correlations with current tokens. We\nassess the effectiveness of this eviction policy through pre-trained OPT-30B and six downstream tasks. The\noutcomes of these evaluations are illustrated in Figure 2. It is obvious that the H2 based eviction policy can\nlargely reduce the KV cache size without degrading the performance of OPT-30B.\nMoreover, during the post analysis, inspired by [35], we find that H2 based policy is related to the classical\ngreedy algorithm (a polynomial-time algorithm with provable guarantees) under the assumption that the\nattention schema is submodular. We present details in Appendix D.\nLemma 3.1 (informal). Assuming the attention scheme is submodular, then greedily constructing the set Si\n(without cache size limitation) satisfies the near-optimal property in terms of submodular.\n4\nHeavy-Hitter Oracle\nThe goal of this section is to propose the greedy algorithm using the H2-based policy and to show the\nprovable guarantees. We first present the H2-based policy called H2O cache eviction policy and formulate its\ndeployment in LLM generation as a variant of submodular maximization problem, named dynamic submodular.\nThen we present H2O in the generative process, followed by a practical example of deploying our proposal.\nFinally, we provide theoretical guarantees for H2O and show our efficient system implementation.\n4.1\nGreedy Algorithm for Low-Cost Policy\nWe have shown a simple yet effective KV cache policy based on H2. However, it is impractical to deploy such\nan algorithm because we do not have access to the future-generated tokens. Fortunately, we empirically\nobserve that local H2, which is calculated using local statistics at every decoding step by summing up the\nattention scores of the previous tokens, is equally effective as taking into account the attention of future\ntokens (Figure 2). In the following, we formally define this dynamic attention score computation (with space\nlimitation) as a novel dynamic submodular type problem.\nDefinition 4.1 (Dynamic submodular framework, informal). Define function F : 2[n] × 2[n] →R, then for\nany set Z ⊂[n], we assume that F(Z, ·) : 2[n] →R is a submodular function w.r.t. to Z, i.e.,\n• For all sets X, Y ⊂[n] satisfy that Z ⊂X ⊂Y ,\n• For all element x ∈[n] satisfy that x ∈[n]\\Y ,\nwe have f(X ∪{x}) −f(X) ≥f(Y ∪{x}) −f(Y ), where f(·) := F(Z, ·).\nRemark 4.2. We provide practical insights of Definition 4.1. X denotes the existing words in the KV cache.\nY is any superset of X. x can be viewed as a “word” which is either newly added to KV cache or existing\ndeleted from KV cache. An example f can be attention score, i.e., see Algorithm 1.\nIf we load the sequence of S1, S2, · · · , Sn (we promise that |Si| ≤k and |Si\\Si−1| ≤1) into Definition 4.1,\ni.e., for each i ∈[n], we choose Z = Si, then it becomes a particular instance of the dynamic submodular\nproblem.\nNext, we provide a formal description of our algorithm, followed by an example.\nDefinition 4.3 (H2O Eviction Policy). Let Fscore : 2[n] →R denote certain score function. Let Si−1 denote\nthe source set. Let Si denote the target set. We defined the eviction policy g : Si−1 →Si s.t.\n• |Si| = k (KV cache size is not changing over the time)\n• |Si\\Si−1| ≤1 or equivalently |Si ∩Si−1| ≥k −1 (we can evict at most 1 KV in the KV cache)\n• We construct Si ←(Si−1 ∪{i})\\{u} as u ←arg maxv∈(Si−1∪{i}) Fscore(Si−1 ∪{i}\\{v}}\n5\n\n\n=== PDF page 6 ===\nTo describe our algorithm (Algorithm 1), we choose a particular instantiation of the function Fscore, i.e.,\nthe summation of that sets in the attention matrix.\nAlgorithm 1 H2 Eviction Algorithm\n1: procedure H2_Eviction(Q, K ∈Rn×d, k ∈N)\n2:\nLet k denote the budget size of cache\n3:\nS0 ←∅\n4:\nfor i = 1 →n do\n5:\nif i ≤k then\n6:\nSi ←Si−1 ∪{i}\n7:\nelse\n8:\nDi ←(exp(Qi,∗(KSi,∗)⊤) −1[i]\\Si) · 1i\n9:\noi ←D−1\ni\n· exp(Qi,∗(KSi,∗)⊤)\n10:\nFscore(T) := P\ns∈T os\n11:\nGi ←Si−1 ∪{i}\n12:\nu ←arg max\nv∈Gi\nFscore(Si−1 ∪{i}\\{v}}\n13:\nSi ←(Si−1 ∪{i})\\{u}\n14:\nend if\n15:\nend for\n16: end procedure\n0.1\n0.1 0.5\nChildren laughed\nand\nthe\nsunny\npark\n...\n.\nplayed\n0.2 0.1 0.1\n1\n0.9\n0.4\n0.03 0.02 0.2 0.05 0.9\n0.6\nin\n1.43\n0.65\n1.52\n0.9\n0.6\n0.03\n0.02\n0.05\n0.9\n0.1\n0.1\n0.5\n0.2\n0.1\n0.1\n1\n0.9\n0.4\n0.2\n0.3 0.01 0.02 0.9\n0.6\n1.6\n0.62\n1.8\n0.9\n0.01\n0.03\n0.04\n0.02\n0.9\n0.01\n0.51\n0.4\n0.1\n0.1\n0.1\n0.5\nChildren laughed\nand\nin\nthe\nsunny\npark\n...\n.\nplayed\n0.2\n0.1\n1\n0.9\n0.6\n0.2\n0.1\n0.1\n0.6\n1.4\n0.6\n1.5\n0.5\n0.4\n0.1\n0.4\n0.1\nQuery\nKey\nValue\nDecoding Step 4\nValue\nKey\n0.03\nQuery\nDecoding Step 5\n0.6\n0.02\nEviction w. Global Statistic \n(infeasible) \nFigure 3: Illustration of Algorithm 1 during two\nconsecutive decoding steps.\nFigure 3 presents an illustrative example of our H2 Eviction Algorithm. We assume that the budget\nsize of KV cache is 3. Following the completion of the fourth decoding step, the KV embeddings associated\nwith the third token are evicted based on the accumulated attention score. Consequently, these evicted KV\nembeddings become inaccessible in the subsequent decoding steps.\n4.2\nTheoretical Guarantee and System Implementation\nWe state a theoretical result as follows. The proofs and more details are provided in Appendix D.\nTheorem 4.4 (informal). Under the mild assumption, let k denote the budget of space limitation. If for each\ntoken, we greedily compute the attention score based on top-k choice, then we can show the set eSi we generate\neach for token i satisfy that f(eSi) ≥(1 −α)(1 −1/e) max|S|=k f(S) −β, where α, β > 0 are parameters.\nRemark 4.5. We remark the above theorem provides a theoretical explanation of why can we hope our greedy\nalgorithm (with cache limitation) can provide a good solution to the problem.\nImplementation Details.\nWe provide a general framework that can support any KV cache eviction\nalgorithm and enhance throughput and reduce the latency of LLM generation with careful implementation.\nFor example, to ensure I/O efficiency, we do not swap memory when stored KV is evicted, but directly fill\nwith newly-added KV. More details are included in Appendix A.\n5\nEmpirical Evaluation\nIn this section, our goal is to demonstrate that H2O, a remarkably simple KV cache eviction policy is capable\nof enhancing end-to-end throughput and reducing latency in wall-clock while maintaining generation quality\nacross a broad spectrum of domains and tasks.\n• In Section 5.1, we show that H2O can reduce the memory footprint of KV cache by up to 5× without\naccuracy degradation on a wide range of model architectures (OPT, LLaMA, GPT-NeoX), sizes (from\n6.7B to 175B) and evaluation benchmarks (HELM and lm-eval-harness). More importantly, can enhance\nthe performance of existing KV cache sparsification techniques.\n6\n\n\n=== PDF page 7 ===\nCOPA, OPT-30B\nMathQA, OPT-30B\nRTE, OPT-66B\nOpenBookQA, OPT-66B\nXSUM, LLaMA-7B\nXSUM, LLaMA-13B\nXSUM, LLaMA-30B\nXSUM, GPT-NeoX-20B\nCNN/Daily Mail, LLaMA-7B\nCNN/Daily Mail, GPT-NeoX-20B\nOpenBookQA, OPT-30B\nCOPA, OPT-66B\nFigure 4: Comparsion results between the baseline model with full cache, our H2O, and the \"Local\" strategy that\nutilizes the most recent KV embeddings.\n• In Section 5.2, we demonstrate that H2O can increase the inference throughput by up to 3×, 29×, 29×\ncompared to the state-of-the-art inference engine FlexGen, DeepSpeed and the widely used Hugging Face\nAccelerate without compromising model quality.\n• In Section 5.3, we present extensive ablation studies to show the effectiveness of H2O under different\nsequence lengths, and its compatibility with quantization.\nAll details (hyperparameters, data splits, etc.), along with additional experiments, are in Appendix A.\n5.1\nEnd-to-End Results\nWe demonstrate that H2O can reduce KV cache memory footprint by 5-10× while achieving comparable\naccuracy on a majority of tasks.\nSetup.\nOur experiments are based on three representative model families of LLMs, including the OPT [36]\nwith model sizes ranging from 6.7 billion to 175 billion, LLaMA [37], and GPT-NeoX-20B [38]. We sample\neight tasks from two popular evaluation frameworks (HELM [15] and lm-eval-harness [14]): COPA [39],\nMathQA [40], OpenBookQA [41], PiQA [42], RTE [43], Winogrande [44], XSUM [45], CNN/Daily Mail [46].\nWe use NVIDIA A100 80GB GPU.\nBaselines.\nSince H2O evenly assigns the caching budget to H2 and the most recent KV, except for full KV\ncache, we consider the \"Local\" strategy as a baseline method. In addition, we also provide two different\nvariants of Sparse Transformers (strided and fixed) as strong baselines.\nMain Results.\nWe evaluate LLMs with KV cache budget ranging from 4% to 100% on 5-shot downstream\ntasks. Results are summarized in Figure 4 and Table 1. The following observations can be drawn: (1)\n7\n\n\n=== PDF page 8 ===\nWith different KV cache budgets, our H2O demonstrates consistent and significant improvements against\nthe \"Local\" strategy across various model sizes, model types, and downstream tasks. We can draw similar\nconclusions comparing H2O with other baselines like Sparse Transformer. Meanwhile, with less than 20% KV\ncache budget(i.e., more than 5× memory reduction), H2O achieves comparable performance as the model\nwith full KV embeddings. Our H2O shows consistent effectiveness in the more challenging long sequence\ngeneration tasks, XSUM, and CNN/Daily Mail.\nTable 1: Results of different sparsification methods w. or w.o. H2\n76.00\n41.40\n77.80\n64.96\nAnalysis.\nSince the evicted KV will not\nbe seen in the future steps, dropping cer-\ntain critical KV embeddings can cause\na severe functional collapse, resulting in\nsignificant performance degradation, e.g.,\nin {LLaMA-13B, XSUM} {LLaMA-7B,\nCNN/Daily Mail}, the \"Local\" strategy\ncollapses at 60% budgets while our H2O\ncan still match the full cache performance\nwith 20% budgets. In some tasks, our\nmethods even surpass the baseline mod-\nels, which demonstrates a regularization\neffect of our H2O. For example, in {OPT-66B, RTE}, {OPT-30B, MathQA} and {GPT-NeoX-20B, XSUM},\nour H2O achieves an extra performance improvement of 0.73%, 0.64% and 0.18 with 20% KV cache budget,\nrespectively. These consistent results validate the effectiveness of our H2O framework.\nEnhancing Baseline Techniques.\nImportantly, we observe other sparsification baselines fail under an\nextremely low cache budget while combining the most recent KV embeddings with the ones of heavy hitters\nsuccessfully achieves comparable performance as using full KV embeddings. From Table 1, we can observe\nthat both \"strided\" and \"fixed\" sparse attention fail under 20% KV cache budgets, encountering a significant\nperformance drop (up to 35% compared with the full cache). After combining with H2, both approaches\nreach a similar performance as using full KV embeddings.\n5.2\nHeavy Hitter for High-Throughput Generative Inference\nTable 2: Generation throughput (token/s) on a T4 GPU with different systems. When\nthe model and KV cache do not fit\n8\n\n\n=== PDF page 9 ===\ninto a single GPU, we turn on CPU offloading. We use synthetic datasets where all prompts are padded\nto the same length. The system is then required to generate the same number of tokens for each prompt.\nWe test different combinations of prompt and generation lengths. The evaluation metric is generation\nthroughput, which is the number of generated tokens / (prompt time + decoding time). We use DeepSpeed\nZeRO-Inference [16], Hugging Face Accelerate [17], and FlexGen [18] as baselines. On the A100 GPU, with\nmore GPU memory, we evaluate the performance of the systems with sequence lengths up to 10K. Although\nOPT is only trained on 2K sequence length, we benchmark the throughput and latency performance to show\nthe potential of H2O for better models in the future.\nResults.\nTable 2 shows the generation throughput of all systems on the T4 GPU. With our KV cache\neviction policy, the memory usage is reduced, which brings two advantages: 1) we can use a much larger batch\nsize; 2) we can make a setting from requiring offloading to not requiring offloading. As shown in Table 2,\nH2O with a 20% budget improves the generation throughput over FlexGen, DeepSpeed, and Accelerate by up\nto 3×, 29×, and 29×, respectively. The results on the A100 GPU with sequence lengths from 4K to 10K\nare listed in Table 3. With the same batch size, H2O can reduce the latency by 1.1 −1.9× compared to\nFlexGen. Additionally, H2O saves memory so it allows a larger batch size, which brings 2.3× improvement\non generation throughput for OPT-6.7B.\n5.3\nAblation Results\nTable 4: Results under different sequence length of OPT-\n30B with 20% KV cache budget.\nTasks\nMethods\n5-shots\n10-shots\nOPT-30B\nOPT-66B\nOPT-30B\nOPT-66B\nOpenBookQA\nFull\n43.20\n44.40\n43.00\n44.80\nLocal\n25.20\n30.60\n26.60\n38.80\nH2O\n43.00\n44.20\n42.80\n44.80\nCOPA\nFull\n85.00\n83.00\n86.00\n85.00\nLocal\n48.00\n59.00\n60.00\n76.00\nH2O\n84.00\n82.00\n85.00\n86.00\nMathQA\nFull\n26.23\n27.87\n26.67\n27.00\nLocal\n20.87\n25.49\n21.11\n23.08\nH2O\n26.87\n27.67\n26.47\n27.30\nWe present extensive ablation studies of H2O on\n(1) different sequence lengths, (2) compatibility with\nquantization methods on KV cache, and (3) dissecting\nthe effectiveness of different components. Moreover,\nwe find a surprising property of H2O – it not only\nimproves the efficiency of LLMs, but also increases\nthe diversity of the generated text.\nQ1: Does the number of shots during in-\nference effects the effectiveness of H2O? A1:\nEffective under both 5 and 10 shots inference.\nWe further examine H2O under different numbers of\nshots during inference, and the results are reported\nin Table 4. Both with 5 and 10 shots inference, our H2O achieves matching performance (difference less than\n1.00%) as the full model across different downstream tasks. And the \"Local\" strategy encounters significant\nperformance degradation (up to 37.00%. Such results demonstrate the effectiveness of our H2O under different\ninference scenarios.\nTable 5: Compatibility with Quantiza-\ntion.\nModels\nCOPA\nOpenBookQA\nPiQA\nFull\n85.00\n43.20\n78.51\nH2O\n84.00\n43.00\n78.45\nQuant-4bit\n84.00\n43.28\n78.67\nH2O w. Quant-4bit\n84.00\n43.20\n78.80\nQ2: Compatible with Quatization? A2: Yes. To pursue\nfurther efficiency, we show the compatibility of H2O with another\northogonal approach, i.e., quantization in Table 5. We use OPT-\n30B as our base model and COPA, OpenBookWA, and PiQA as\nevaluation tasks. Intuitively sparsity and quantization are highly\nrelated so combining them might introduce larger errors. Surprisingly\nthe combination almost always achieves better accuracy than H2O\nor quantization alone. Experiments about throughput improvement\nof H2O combining with quantization see Appendix C.\nQ3: When does H2O match the baseline with full KV embeddings? A3: With both H2 and\nthe recent tokens. We investigate the separate effects of KV embeddings of H2 and the local tokens. We\nconduct experiments on 4 tasks with OPT-13B and OPT-30B. For each task, we compare the performance of\nthree KV cache eviction policies, including only the KV embeddings of H2, only the ones of local tokens, and\nour H2O that keep both. As shown in Table 6, only retaining the embeddings of H2 or local tokens can’t\nmaintain a similar performance as the model using full embeddings, with a performance degradation from\n2.85% to 22.75%. Incorporating both components, our H2O successfully retains the baseline performance with\nfull embeddings. Besides, the model with only H2 shows a consistent improvement against the one with only\nlocal tokens, which indicates that H2 might contribute more to maintaining the performance of networks.\n9\n\n\n=== PDF page 10 ===\nTable 6: Ablation study of H2O across different tasks.\nTasks\nModels\nFull\nw. Besides all\nthe benefits of our H2O, we also observe an bonus\nintroduced by H2O, i.e., the improved diversity of\ngenerated content. The results are reported in Ap-\npendix C. Given the same prompts, we visualize the\ngenerated text of the models with different KV cache\nbudgets. Compared with the model of full KV cache,\nour H2O can generate sentences with fewer repeated\nwords and more creativity.\n6\nConclusion and Discussion\nIn this paper, we study one of the key bottlenecks of LLM deployment, KV cache, particularly for long-content\nand large-batch generation applications. We propose H2O, a simple KV cache eviction policy for significantly\nreducing its memory footprint. The main insight of our approach is the recognition of a subset of tokens,\nknown as Heavy Hitters, which contribute the most value when computing attention scores. We formulate the\nKV cache eviction as a dynamic submodular problem and provide the theoretical guarantees for our algorithm.\nThrough extensive evaluations, we demonstrate that H2O can significantly improve end-to-end throughput\nand decrease latency in wall-clock time, without compromising the generation quality of LLMs across a\nvariety of tasks.\n10\n\n\n=== PDF page 11 ===\nReferences\n[1] Romal Thoppilan, Daniel De Freitas, Jamie Hall, Noam Shazeer, Apoorv Kulshreshtha, Heng-Tze Cheng,\nAlicia Jin, Taylor Bos, Leslie Baker, Yu Du, et al. . . . . . . . . . . . . .\n44\n17\n\n\n=== PDF page 18 ===\nA\nMore Implementation Details\nIn this section, our goal is to provide the details of system implementation (mentioned in Section 4.2) and\nexperiment settings (mentioned in Section 5), as well as the pseudocode.\nSystem Details.\nWe implement H2O on top of FlexGen. FlexGen is a white-box implementation of OPT\nmodels, and we have done some surgery on handling the KV cache. Specifically, for the given parameter k, we\nalways maintain a list of KV cache with the first k entries as heavy hitters, and the last k entries as most\nrecent tokens. In order to avoid data movement in memory, the memory for KV cache is preallocated. We use\na circular queue to efficiently update the last k entries.\nExperiment Details.\nOur study involves the evaluation with varying sizes of KV cache that encompass\n4%, 10%, 20%, and 60% of the prompt’s length. We select two tasks (XSUM and CNN/Daily Mail) from the\nHELM framework [15] and present the performance based on 1000 test samples. Additionally, we employ\nthe lm-eval-harness framework [14] for six other tasks (COPA, MathQA, OpenBookQA, PiQA, RTE, and\nWinogrande). For the tasks derived from the HELM framework, we report the performance for zero-shot\ninference, while for the tasks from lm-eval-harness, we default to conduct five-shot inference.\nPseudocode.\nWe show some pseudocode to demonstrate our implementation skeleton. The function\ngeneration_loop() is the base loop in FlexGen that controls prefetch and overlap the I/O streams and\ncomputation. Then in function compute_layer(), the function attention_forward() will be called for\nattention layers. The function compute_attention() will return new KV cache and the indices for evicted\nentries during decoding iterations, which would be the place to implement a customized evict strategy. During\neach decoding iteration, the oldest one among the last k tokens will be removed from the reserved last k\nentries, one heavy hitter will be evicted for each head, and the newest token will be added to the KV cache\nwith a position in the last k entries. This happens in the function store_cache().\ndef\ngeneration_loop (...):\n# Prologue\n...\n# Generate\nfor i in range(gen_len):\nfor j in range(num_layers):\nfor k in range( num_gpu_batches ):\nload_weight(i, j+1, k)\nload_cache(i, j, k+1)\nstore_hidden (i, j, k -1)\nload_hidden(i, j, k+1)\ncompute_layer (i, j, k)\nstore_cache(i, j, k-1)\nsync ()\n# Epilogue\n...\n# h is the\nhidden\nstates (activations )\ndef\nattention_forward (h, ...):\n# The\nread/write\nbuffer\nare\nintermediate\nstops\nfor\nprefetching\nif\nprefill:\nh, new_k_cache , new_v_cache = compute_attention (h, ...)\ncache_write_buffer .store(new_k_cache , new_v_cache )\nelse:\nk_cache , v_cache = cache_read_buf .pop ()\n# evict_ids\ntrack\nthe\nentries\nthat\nwill be\nevicted\nh, new_k_cache , new_v_cache , evict_ids =\ncompute_attention (h, k_cache , v_cache , ...)\ncache_write_buffer .store(new_k_cache , new_v_cache , evict_ids)\nreturn h\ndef\nstore_cache (...):\nif\nprefill:\n# store\ncache\ndirectly\n18\n\n\n=== PDF page 19 ===\n...\nelse:\nk_new , v_new , evict_ids = cache_write_buffer .pop ()\n# circular\nqueue\nfor the\nlast K entries\n# extract\nthe\nindex\nfor the\noldest\ntoken at i-th\niteration\noldest = ((i - 1) % K) - K\n# update\nthe KV cache (k_home\nand\nv_home)\ncache_replace (k_home , evict_ids , k_new , K, oldest)\ncache_replace (v_home , evict_ids , v_new , K, oldest)\nB\nExtended Related Works, Discussions, and Limitations\nThe goal of this section is to first introduce more background and related works for Section 2, then describe\nsome previous attempts in our experiments as well as discuss the social impact and limitations of this work.\nB.1\nExtended Related Works\nEfficient Inference of LLMs.\nThe substantial parameter counts of large language models (LLMs) present\nsignificant challenges for inference. To overcome this limitation, previous efforts have employed model\ncompression techniques with specific designs to achieve efficient LLM inference, such as the method described\nin [47], which employs one-shot pruning on LLMs (e.g., OPT-175B and BLOOM-176B), resulting in negligible\nperformance degradation even without retraining. Additionally, alternative approaches explore quantization\nmethods specifically tailored to LLMs, as discussed in [48, 49, 50, 51, 52]. These methods address efficient\ninference from orthogonal perspectives and can be organically integrated. The techniques investigated in this\nstudy are closely associated with pruning or sparsity but focus on a distinct inference bottleneck, namely,\nKV cache. One closely related work[53] utilizes a learnable mechanism that determines necessary tokens\nduring inference but requires an extra fine-tuning process, which makes it less practical.\nQuantization, Pruning, Distillation for Inference.\nPreviously, model compression algorithms have been\nextensively investigated as a viable approach for mitigating the computational resource requirements of model\ninference. These algorithms can be broadly categorized into three groups: (1) quantization [19, 20, 21, 22],\nwhich involves mapping model parameters or activations from high-precision data types to low-precision\ncounterparts, such as using 8-bit integers instead of the commonly employed 32-bit floating point format;\n(2) pruning or sparsity [23, 24, 25, 26], which aims to eliminate unnecessary neurons or weights within the\nmodels; (3) and distillation [27, 28, 29, 30] where predictions from larger models are utilized as supervised\ninformation to train smaller models.\nTransformer in NLP.\nTransformers [54] as a popular option have been frequently adopted by plenty of\nnatural language processing (NLP) applications with prevailing successes [55, 56, 57, 58, 59, 43, 60, 12, 61, 62].\nRoughly, modern transformer-based networks can be categorized into two groups: (1) Encoder-Decoder or\nEncoder-only (i.e., BERT-style models [63]). This type of transformers commonly leverages the Masked\nLanguage Modeling task which encourages models to capture the intrinsic relationship between words and\ntheir context. Notable examples include BERT [63], RoBBERTa [56] and T5 [64]. (2) Decoder-only (i.e.,\nGPT-style models [65]). Usually, this group of transformers adopts the Casual Language Modeling task,\nwhich is optimized to generate the next word/token in a sequence based on the preceding words/tokens. Such\nan autoregressive manner is highly preferred by downstream tasks like text generation and question answering.\nGPT-3 [66], OPT [36], PaLM [12], and BLOOM [67] are representative architectures within this huge family.\nTraining of Transformer.\nTraining a gigantic transformer-based model is not trivial. It notoriously\nsuffers from various issues such as overfitting, instability, etc. [68, 69, 70] analyze these bottlenecks from the\noptimization perspective. To address the issues, a great amount of pioneering effort is devoted, including\ndata augmentations [71, 72], a better initialization [73, 70, 74, 75], customized optimizers [76], improved\nnormalization [77, 78], weight decay [79], and early stopping. However, there is still a long way to go before\nwe can fully clarify the mystery of transformer training.\n19\n\n\n=== PDF page 20 ===\nSparse, Low-rank Attention Approx.\nThe quadratic computational complexity of attention modules\nis one of the major bottlenecks of transformer inference [31]. Various efforts are devoted to addressing this\nchallenge [7, 10, 9]. For example, Reformer [7] reduces the computational cost from quadratic to superlinear\ncomplexity via locality-sensitive hashing. Performer [9] employs positive orthogonal random features to\napproximate attention kernels. The most relevant work, Sparse Transformer [10], introduces sparsity to\nreduce KV cache memory footprint and achieve an efficient attention mechanism, considered as our baseline\nin this paper.\nB.2\nDiscussions and Limitations\nPrevious Attempts.\nDuring our experiments, we find several noteworthy observations. In H2O, employing\nthe accumulated attention score to evict KV embeddings can lead to a potential bias favoring the least recent\ntokens. This bias arises because most previous tokens have a higher number of attention scores, resulting in a\nhigher accumulated attention score and, consequently, a greater likelihood of being retained. To address this\nconcern, we conducted an additional experiment utilizing the averaged attention score to determine which\nKV embeddings should be retained. However, this alternative approach resulted in performance degradation.\nAdditionally, we observed a significant proportion of H2 occurrences at the beginning of sentences. This\nfinding suggests that the initial tokens play a substantial role in subsequent generation tasks.\nSocial Impact.\nOur work represents an initial effort in designing a KV Cache policy, a realm that has been\nrelatively unexplored and yet is a significant bottleneck in LLMs. The proposed Heavy Hitter Oracle (H2O)\nprovide a solution to improve the efficiency of LLM generation, which can save energy cost and contribute to\ngreen AI. Besides, our approach also serves as a source of inspiration for future advanced algorithm designs.\nWe envision H2O as a foundational framework that could facilitate further innovation in this area. Moreover,\nlong content generation is an area of growing importance that currently grapples with several efficiency\nissues. We hope our work that supports the generation of very long sequences will support further research\nin this direction, particularly in terms of enhancing consistency, devising superior evaluation methods, and\nestablishing robust benchmarks.\nFurthermore, another contribution of this study is the formulation of a dynamic submodular framework.\nWe believe that this theoretical framework possesses the potential to be applicable beyond specific domains\nof interest. For instance, there may exist numerous other dynamic problems where the task involves solving a\nsubmodular problem with slight variations at each time.\nLimitations.\nFurthermore, despite the notable advancements in throughput of our H2O, implementing\nLLMs for generative inference remains challenging due to the immense parameter count. As a substantial\nportion of these parameters is encompassed within the MLP blocks, building upon our observations of H2\noccurrences in the MLP blocks, future research efforts can be directed towards leveraging the characteristics\nof H2 to devise an offloading policy. Such a policy can potentially enhance the efficiency of LLM inference\neven further.\n20\n\n\n=== PDF page 21 ===\nC\nExtended Experiments\nIn this section, our goal is to demonstrate that H2O can improve the diversity of generated text (mentioned in\nSection 5.3) and throughput is further improved when combined with quantization (mentioned in Section 5.3).\nMoreover, additional investigations about H2 are reported, including H2O can also enhance the \"Top-K\"\nbaseline; extra results of H2 in attention blocks; the emergence of H2 in MLP blocks as well as its properties.\nC.1\nIncreased Diversity of Generated Text\nModel Input\nIn the year 2087, humanity has achieved remarkable technological advancements and established colonies on\nmultiple planets within the Milky Way galaxy. H2\n82.00\n42.80\n77.96\n66.48\nC.4\nHeavy-Hitter in Attention Blocks\nThe distribution of accumulated attention scores of all the tokens within attentions blocks is illustrated in\nFigure 7. We can observe that H2 broadly exists in each layer.\nC.5\nHeavy-Hitter in MLP Blocks\nBesides the attention blocks, the presence of Heavy-Hitters (H2) is observed within the MLP blocks of LLMs.\nWe utilize the Wiki-Text-103 dataset as the input and record the activated frequency of neurons in the\nhidden layer of MLP blocks. As depicted in Figure 8, the activated frequency of neurons follows a power-law\ndistribution, wherein a small number of neurons are activated by nearly all input tokens (with a 100%\nfrequency) while the majority of other neurons are rarely activated.\nSubsequently, a thorough examination of various characteristics pertaining to H2 in MLP blocks is\nconducted, encompassing the following aspects: (1) The elimination of H2 leads to a substantial decline in\nperformance, although such degradation can be easily recovered even with a mere 1% of the training data;\n(2) H2 exhibits a significant degree of overlap across different type of input content; (3) The emergence of\nH2 occurs early in the training process, thus exhibiting an \"early-bird\" characteristic, and their positions\nundergo gradual changes during subsequent training phases.\nElimination of H2.\nWe first train a GPT-2 using Wiki-Text-103 dataset and subsequently identify and\nprune the neurons exhibiting an activation frequency exceeding 20% (i.e., H2). This pruning operation leads\nto a substantial decline in performance, as evidenced by an increase in perplexity from 19.32 to 31.78. The\nresults emphasize the criticality of H2 in preserving the functionality of the model. To assess the recoverability\nof the discarded information, we conduct a few-shot fine-tuning experiment, and the results are summarized\nin Table 9. The pruned model is fine-tuned with varying ratios of training data for 500 iterations, and\nit successfully regains performance levels equivalent to those of the pre-trained model. In contrast, when\ntraining the model from scratch using only 1% of the training data, the resulting model achieves a perplexity\nof 554.12 only. These findings demonstrate that the knowledge encoded in H2 can be easily restored.\nTable 9: . Perplexity on the test-set of Wiki-Text-3 with GPT-2.\nSettings\n1%\n10%\n40%\n100%\nPretrained Model\n19.32\nRemove H2\n31.78\nFine-tuning\n19.86\n19.84\n19.76\n19.83\nOverlap across Diverse Input Contents.\nMoreover, we conduct a comparative analysis of the activation\nfrequencies acquired from various input contents. Specifically, utilizing the pretrained OPT-1.3B model, we\nevaluate three datasets, namely Wiki-Text-103, Penn Treebank, and Amazon Review. The positioning of H2\nis depicted in Figure 9, revealing significant concurrence across multiple datasets.\n23\n\n\n=== PDF page 24 ===\n0\n10000 20000 30000 40000 By visualizing the distribution of activation frequencies\nacross various checkpoints throughout the training process, we\nobserve the emergence of a power-law behavior at an initial stage,\nspecifically as early as a training budget of 4%. Subsequently, the\npositions of H2 exhibit gradual and minimal changes.\n27\n\n\n=== PDF page 28 ===\nD\nTheoretical Analysis\nRecently, a number of works have studied the attention scheme in LLMs from a theoretical perspective\n[81, 82, 83, 84, 85, 86, 87, 88, 89]. In this work, we provide a different and novel angle compared to the previous\nwork. We present the concept of the submodular property and propose an eviction policy, known as greedy\nH2, which is a modification of dynamic submodular maximization. Furthermore, assuming the attention\nscheme to be submodular, we establish that constructing the set Si without any cache size limitation satisfies\nthe near-optimal property in terms of submodularity. We provide theoretical guarantees for our robust and\napproximate greedy eviction policy algorithm (Algorithm 2). Due to space limitation, we only give informal\ndescription of algorithm (Algorithm 2) in Section 4.1 In Section D.6, we give an algorithm (Algorithm 2)\nwhich has full and complete implementation details for Algorithm 1. We also offer a mathematical formulation\nfor sparsity preservation that is observed in Section 3 and proposed an algorithm (Algorithm 4) to solve the\nproblem.\nSpecifically, in Section D.1, we provide several basic definitions and notations. In Section D.2, we briefly\nthe definition of submodular function. In Section D.3, we define the dynamic submodular framework, which\ngives the formal version of Definition 4.1. In Section D.4, we briefly review the static attention computation\nproblem. In Section D.5, we formulate the attention computation in recursive fashion. In Section D.6, we\nbriefly review our eviction policy, which gives the formal version of Definition 4.3. In Section D.7, we discuss\nthe diminishing return for submodular. In Section D.8, we discuss the high-level ideas for submodular. In\nSection D.9, we analyze the robust greedy algorithm error propagation. In Section D.10, we explain how to add\nitems into sets via approximate function. In Section D.11, we provide several definitions related to dynamic\nproperties. In Section D.12, we prove an induction lemma for the exact function. In Section D.13, we prove an\ninduction lemma for the approximation function. In Section D.14, we provide theoretical guarantees for both\nthe full-knowledge version (formal version of Lemma 3.1) and the limited-cache-size version (formal version\nof Theorem 4.4). In Section D.15, we provide a more detailed discussion of theoretical work about attention\ncomputation and regression-related problems. In Section D.16, we provide a mathematical formulation for\nsparsity preserving. In Section D.17, we provide the definition of loss function which can potentially generate\nsparse (heavy hitter type attention sore). In Section D.18, we explain how to compute the gradient of the\nloss function. In Section D.19, we show how to compute the Hessian of the loss function. In Section D.20,\nwe show that Hessian is positive definite. In Section D.21, we prove the Lipschitz property for the Hessian\nmatrix. In Section D.22, we show that using a gradient-type algorithm is sufficient to optimize that (heavy\nhitter type) loss function.\nD.1\nNotations\nFor a positive integer n, let [n] := {1, 2, · · · , n}.\nFor a vector x ∈Rn, let √x ∈Rn denote the vector with the i-th entry being √xi and diag(x) ∈Rn×n\ndenote the diagonal matrix with the i-th digonal entry being xi. For two matrices A, W ∈Rn×n, let\n∥A∥W := (Pn\ni=1\nPn\nj=1 Wi,jA2\ni,j)1/2 and W ◦A denote the matrix where (W ◦A)i,j = Wi,jAi,j. For matrix\nW ∈Rn×n, let DWi := diag(Wi,:) with i ∈[n].\nFor two vectors x ∈Rn and w ∈Rn\n≥0, let ∥x∥w := (Pn\ni=1 wix2\ni )1/2. For a vector x, its ℓ2 norm is defined\nas ∥x∥2 := (Pn\ni=1 x2\ni )1/2 and its ℓp norm is defined as ∥x∥p := (Pn\ni=1 |xi|p)1/p. For a square matrix A, we\ndenote tr[A] as the trace of matrix A.\nFor a matrix A ∈Rn×k (suppose n ≥k), we use ∥A∥to denote its spectral norm, i.e., ∥A∥=\nsupx ∥Ax∥2/∥x∥2. We use ∥A∥F to denote its Frobenius norm ∥A∥F := (Pn\ni=1\nPk\nj=1 A2\ni,j)1/2.\nSuppose matrix A ∈Rn×k has SVD decomposition UΣV ⊤where U ∈Rn×k (this matrix has orthonormal\ncolumns), Σ ∈Rk×k is a diagonal matrix, and V ∈Rk×k. We call columns of U singular vectors. We use\nA† ∈Rk×n to denote the Moore-Penrose pseudoinverse, then A† = V Σ−1U ⊤. Suppose Σ ∈Rk×k is sorted\ndiagonal matrix, let σ1, · · · , σk denote the diagonal entries of Σ. Then we call σi the i-th singular value of\nthe matrix, and we write it as σi(A).\nFor any symmetric matrix B ∈Rk×k, we denote its eigenvalue decomposition as UΛU ⊤, where Λ is a\ndiagonal matrix. Let λ1, · · · , λk denote the entries on diagonal of Λ ∈Rk×k. We say λi is the i-th eigenvalue.\nUsually, we write it as λi(B).\n28\n\n\n=== PDF page 29 ===\nThe connection between eigenvalues and singular values is\nσ2\ni (A) = λi(A⊤A)\nWe use the notation A ⪰0 to denote that matrix A is positive semidefinite (psd). Mathematically, A ⪰0\nmeans for all vectors x, we have x⊤Ax ≥0.\nSimilarly, for two squarer matrices A and B, we use A ⪰B to denote the case where for all vectors x,\nx⊤Ax ≥x⊤Bx.\nLet Pr[] and E[] denote the probability and expectation. We define the maximum between a and b as\nmax{a, b}. We denote min{a, b} (resp. max{a, b}) as the minimum (reps. maximum) between a and b.\nThroughout, for non-negative real numbers a and b, we use the notation a = (1±ϵ)b if a ∈[(1−ϵ)b, (1+ϵ)b].\nD.2\nSubmodular\nWe provide the standard definition of the submodular function.\nDefinition D.1 (Submodular function [90]). For a finite set Ω, a submodular function is defined as f : 2Ω→R,\nwhere 2Ωdenotes the power set of Ω. It is characterized by the fulfillment of any of the following equivalent\ncriteria:\n• Condition 1.\n– For S, T, ⊆Ωwith S ⊆T and every a ∈Ω\\T we have that f(S ∪{a}) −f(S) ≥f(T ∪{a}) −f(T)\n• Condition 2.\n– For every S, T ⊆Ωwe have that f(S) + f(T) ≥f(S ∪T) + f(S ∩T).\n• Condition 3.\n– For every S ⊆Ωand a1, a2 ∈Ω\\S such that a1 ̸= a2 we have that f(S ∪{a1}) + f(S ∪{a2}) ≥\nf(S ∪{a1, a2}) + f(S).\nFor convenience of discussion, in this paper, we always choose Ω= [n] when we want to discuss the\nsubmodular function.\nNext, we provide some examples/types of submodular functions. One important class is called monotone,\nDefinition D.2 (Monotone). A set function f is monotone if for every T ⊆S we have f(T) ≤f(S).\nHere are a number of monotone submodular functions\n• Linear (Modular) functions\n– A linear function can be represented as f(S) = P\ni∈S wi. If all the weights wi are nonnegative,\nthen the function f is considered monotone.\n• Budget-additive functions\n– A budget-additive function has the form f(S) = min B, P\ni∈S wi where each weight wi and the\nbudget B are nonnegative.\n• Coverage functions\n– We have a set Ω= {E1, E2, · · · , En} where each Ei is a subset of a broader set Ω′. The coverage\nfunction can be expressed as f(S) = | ∪Ei∈S Ei| for any S ⊂Ω. This function can be generalized\nby assigning non-negative weights to the elements.\n29\n\n\n=== PDF page 30 ===\nD.3\nDynamic Submodular\nThe standard submodular function is mapping 2[n] to real. Here we need a more general definition that maps\n2[n] × 2[n] to real.\nDefinition D.3 (Strong submodular). We define function F : 2[n] × 2[n] →R, then for any set Z ⊂[n], we\nassume that F(Z, ·) : 2[n] →R is a submodular function.\nIn fact, the above definition is stronger than we want, what we really need can be written as follows. We\nremark that in Definition 4.1 we provide an informal definition. Here we provide a more detailed version of\nthe definition.\nDefinition D.4 (Dynamic submodular framework, formal version of Definition 4.1). Define function\nF : 2[n] × [n] × 2[n] →R.\nThen for any index i ∈[n], any set Z ⊆[i −1], we assume that\nF(Z, i, ·) : 2[n] →R\nis a submodular function w.r.t. to Z, i.e.,\n• For all sets X, Y ⊂[n] satisfy that Z ⊂X ⊂Y ,\n• For all element x ∈[n] satisfy that x ∈[n]\\Y ,\nwe have\nfZ,i(X ∪{x}) −fZ,i(X) ≥fZ,i(Y ∪{x}) −fZ,i(Y ),\nwhere fZ,i(·) := F(Z, i, ·).\nRemark D.5. We remark that Definition D.4 is a weaker version of Definition D.3. We also like to mention\nthat the informal definition (see Definition 4.1) only contains two input parameters, but in fact we need three\ninput parameters (see Definition D.4).\nIn the later, when we use fZ,i(·), we will replace Z by Si for convenient of analysis, for example see\nDefinition D.23, Definition D.24, Definition D.25 and Definition D.26.\nD.4\nStatic Attention\nBefore we describe the recursive attention computation, we will first describe the static version of attention\ncomputation as follows (for examples, see Definition 1.1 in [82] and others [81, 83, 84, 85]):\nDefinition D.6. Given three matrices Q, K, V ∈Rd×d, the goal is to compute\nAtt(Q, K, V ) := D−1A · V\nwhere square matrix A ∈Rn×n can be rewritten as follows\nA = exp(QK⊤)\nand diagonal matrix D ∈Rn×n can be written as follows\nD = diag(A1n)\nHere we apply exp() to a matrix entry-wisely to a matrix. 1n is a length-n vector where all the entries are\nones. The operator diag() is turning a vector into a diagonal matrix.\n30\n\n\n=== PDF page 31 ===\no1,1\no2,1\no3,1\no4,1\no2,2\no3,2\no4,2\no3,3\no4,3\no4,4\nI (Q1,∗)\neat (Q2,∗)\nan (Q3,∗)\napple (Q4,∗)\n→\ntoday\nI\neat\nan\napple today\n(K1,∗) (K2,∗) (K3,∗) (K4,∗)\n(a)\no1,1\no2,1\no3,1\no4,1\no2,2\no3,2\no4,2\no3,3\no4,3\no4,4\nI (Q1,∗)\neat (Q2,∗)\nan (Q3,∗)\napple (Q4,∗)\n→\ntoday\nI\neat\nan\napple today\n\u0018\u0018\u0018\n(K1,∗) (K2,∗) (K3,∗) (K4,∗)\n(b)\nFigure 11: (a) Exact version of the attention computation. We start by giving a process without\nhaving space limitations. We denote the attention query matrix as Q ∈Rn×d and the key matrix as K ∈Rn×d.\nQi,∗represents the i-th row of Q and K≤i,∗represents the first i rows of K. For simplicity, KSi,∗denotes a\nsub-matrix of K which selects Si rows from K.\nDefinition D.11 (The generative process with Full knowledge). For each i ∈[n], for the i-th token, we have\n• length-i vector oi := D−1\ni\n· exp(Qi,∗(K≤i,∗)⊤), which denotes the attention of the i-th word.\n• scalar Di := exp(Qi,∗(K≤i,∗)⊤) · 1i, which denotes the normalization factor.\nIn the above process, since we have no space limitation, then we can keep all the scores. Let\nh : R →R (the h being used Line 10 in Algorithm 2) denote function. We hope to choose h such that it gives\nthe submodular property for the function Fscore in the sense of our dynamic framework.
