Thus, a straightforward and better implementation for only using one length-n to store\naccumulative score can reduce n2 usage to n.\nD.7\nExplaining Submodular Diminishing Return Property in Attention Scheme\nIn the standard submodular problem [90], we are given a ground set [n] and a function f : 2[n] →R. The\ngoal is to find a set of elements S such that f(S) is maximized.\n33\n\n\n=== PDF page 34 ===\nWe say function f is submodular (Recall the formal definition in Definition D.1), if for every X, Y ⊂[n]\nwith X ⊆Y and every x ∈[n]\\Y we have\nf(X ∪{x}) −f(X) ≥f(Y ∪{x}) −f(Y )\nSubmodular functions can represent the cost of items, due to their diminishing returns property [94, 95].\nIt suggests that the increase in information obtained from selecting a candidate object, such as a word or\nsentence, becomes smaller as more objects have already been chosen for the summary.\nFor instance, we introduce a new token, denoted as “w”, into two sets, S and S0, where the concepts\ncovered by S0 are a subset of those covered by S. By intuition, the information added to S0 by “w” should be\nlarger compared to adding it to S, as the new concepts carried by “w” might have already been covered by the\nconcepts present in S but not in S0. This property is known as the diminishing return property. Hence, we\npropose that the neural coverage function [96] should exhibit a desirable characteristic called submodularity.\nD.8\nSubmodular: High Level Ideas\nWe formalize the submodular function maximization problem with cardinality constraint in this section.\nInformally, a set function is submodular if it has decreasing marginal increment.\nDefinition D.16 (Submodular function). We denote f : 2[n] →R as a set function. The discrete derivative\n∆f is defined as follows:\n∆f(i | S) := f(S ∪{i}) −f(S).\nA function f is submodular if, for any S ⊆T and i ∈[n]\\T, the following inequality holds:\n∆f(i | T) ≤∆f(i | S).\nFor simplicity, we present the problem of maximizing a submodular function with a cardinality constraint\n(1). Our goal is to solve the optimization problem efficiently.\nmax\nS⊆[n]\nf(S)\ns.t.\n|S| ≤k\n(1)\nRepresentation of f(S)\nOne challenge in designing the algorithm is determining the representation of\ninput instances. Since the constraint in optimization problem (1) is straightforward, we need to decide how to\nrepresent f(S). Suppose S = i1, i2, · · · , im ⊆[n], we can decompose f(S) into a sum of increments as follows:\nf(S) = f(S0) +\nm\nX\nj=1\n(f(Sj) −f(Sj−1)),\n(2)\nwhere S0 = ∅and Sj = Sj−1 + ij. Without loss of generality, we assume f(∅) = 0. By the definition of\n∆f(i|S), we have f(Sj) −f(Sj−1) = ∆f(ij|Sj−1). Therefore, the decomposition (2) can be simplified as\nfollows:\nf(S) =\nm\nX\nj=1\n∆f(ij|Sj−1)\n(3)\nTo introduce our advanced data structure later, we further represent ∆f(i|S) in the form of\n∆f(i|S) = u⊤\ni h(S)ui\n(4)\nwhere ui ∈Rd is a d-dimensional vector and h(S) ∈Rd×d is a d-by-d matrix.\nIn practice, a significant subclass of submodular functions is the monotone submodular functions, i.e.\nfunctions f satisfying f(A) ≤f(B) for all A ⊆B ⊆[n]. When f is monotone, we could restrict all h(S) to\nbe positive semidefinite (PSD) matrixes. When the matrix h(S) is positive semidefinite (PSD), it makes us\nachieve a faster acceleration.\n34\n\n\n=== PDF page 35 ===\nD.9\nRobust Greedy with Error Propagation\nThe procedure for the greedy selection algorithm initiates with empty set S0 = ∅. For each iteration in the\nmain loop, the algorithm chooses the element that maximizes the marginal increment to add to the set.\nWhen the size of the set eventually reaches k, the algorithm return that set S. Specifically, for iteration\nt ∈{1, 2, · · · , k}, we let\nSt ←St−1 ∪{jt}\nwhere the element in the singleton is jt = arg maxj∈St−1 f(St−1 ∪{j}). The greedy strategy is effective in\nthe sense that the approximation error of it is 1 −1/e.\nTheorem D.17 ([97]). For a monotone submodular function f, the greedy algorithm (Algorithm 3) guarantees\nto output a set S satisfying\nf(S) ≥(1 −1/e) max\n|T |=k{f(T)}.\nAlgorithm 3 Greedy algorithm benchmark\n1: procedure GreedyAlgorithm(submodular function f)\n2:\nS0 ←∅\n▷Initialize an empty set S0\n3:\nfor t = 0 →k −1 do\n4:\nj ←arg maxi{f(St ∪{i})}\n▷Find the element i ∈[n] that maximize f(St ∪{i})\n5:\nSt+1 ←St ∪{j}\n▷Append element i to set St to get St+1\n6:\nend for\n7:\nreturn Sk\n8: end procedure\nCorollary D.18 ([98]). Given\n• accuracy parameter ϵ > 0.\n• integer k ≥1\n• Let O denote an oracle that takes an arbitrary set S ⊆[n] and i ∈[n]\\S, returns a value O(S, i) with\nguarantee that ∆(i|S) −ϵ ≤O(S, i) ≤∆(i|S) + ϵ.\n• Let A denote an algorithm that each time step t = 1, 2, · · · , k, it selects jt = arg maxj{O(St−1, j)} and\nlets St ←St−1 ∪{jt}.\nThen this algorithm A\n• returns a set Sk\n• the Sk satisfy that\nf(Sk) ≥(1 −1/e) max\n|T |=k{f(T)} −k(2 −1/e)ϵ.\nD.10\nRobust Submodular and Adding Items\nWe first propose a lemma that shows the robustness of the submodular.\nLemma D.19. Suppose that for each i ∈[n], we have\n• for all Si ⊂[n] with |Si| ≤k\n• for all X ⊂[n], X ⊆Si ∪{i} and |X ∩Si| ≤|Si|,\n|( efSi,i(X)) −efSi,i(Si) −(fSi,i(X) −fSi,i(Si))| ≤ϵ/(2n)\n35\n\n\n=== PDF page 36 ===\nLet opti denote the optimal cost that can be achieved by using f in i-th iteration. If the greedy algorithm use\nf to find the solution has performance at least (1 −1/e) · opti, then using ef, we can obtain a solution that\nhas performance at least (1 −1/e) · opti −ϵ\nProof. The proof follows from Lemma D.18.\nNext, we explain how to add items into sets based on exact values.\nDefinition D.20 (Expanding items based on exact value). If the following conditions hold\n• Let Si ⊆[i −1].\n• Let fSi,i : 2[i] →R.\nThen, we can define Si+1 as follows\n• If |Si| = k then Si+1 = Si ∪{i}\\u where u = arg maxv∈Si∪{i} fSi,i(Si ∪{i}\\v) −fSi,i(Si)\n• If |Si| < k, then Si+1 = Si ∪{i}.\nRemark D.21. We remark that u = arg maxv∈Si∪{i} fSi,i(Si ∪{i}\\v) −fSi,i(Si) is the same as u =\narg maxv∈Si∪{i} fSi,i(Si ∪{i}\\v). For the convenience of discussion and analysis, we will switch to using both\ncases in different places.\nHere, we explain how to add items into sets via approximate values.\nDefinition D.22 (Expanding items based on approximate value). If the following conditions hold\n• Let Si ⊆[i −1].\n• Let efSi,i : 2[i] →R.\nThen, we can define Si+1 as follows\n• If |Si| = k then Si+1 = Si ∪{i}\\u where u = arg maxv∈Si∪{i} efSi,i(Si ∪{i}\\v) −efSi,i(Si)\n• If |Si| < k, then Si+1 = Si ∪{i}.\nD.11\nUniversal Conditions\nWe state several definitions for both the exact function and approximation function.\nDefinition D.23 (Universal Monotone Condition (for exact function)). We say f has universal monotone\ncondition, if for all i ∈[n] for all Si ⊆[i −1], we have\nfSi,i(X) ≥fSi,i(Y ),\n∀Y ⊂X\nDefinition D.24 (Universal Monotone Condition (for approximate function)). We say ef has universal\nmonotone condition, if for all i ∈[n] for all Si ⊆[i −1], we have\nefSi,i(X) ≥efSi,i(Y ),\n∀Y ⊂X\nDefinition D.25 (Universal Dynamic Condition 1(for exact function)). We say f has universal dynamic\ncondition 1(for exact function), if for all i ∈[n] for all Si ⊆[i −1], Si−1 ⊆[i −2], |Si\\Si−1| ≤1, we have\nfSi,i(Si) ≥(1 −θ) · fSi−1,i−1(Si)\nDefinition D.26 (Universal Dynamic Condition 1 (for approximate function)). We say ef has universal\ndynamic condition 1(for approximate function), if for all i ∈[n] for all Si ⊆[i−1], Si−1 ⊆[i−2], |Si\\Si−1| ≤1,\nwe have\nefSi,i(Si) ≥(1 −θ) · efSi−1,i−1(Si)\n36\n\n\n=== PDF page 37 ===\nWe define opti as follows:\nDefinition D.27. Let k denote the budget length. For each i ∈[n], we define opti as\nmax{fX,i(Y ) | X ⊆[i −1], Y ⊆[i], |X| ≤k, |Y | ≤k, |Y \\X| ≤1}.\nDefinition D.28 (Universal Dynamic Condition 2). For i ∈[n], we define opti as Definition D.27. We say\nit has universal dynamic condition if for each i ∈[n], we have\nopti ≥(1 −γ) · opti−1.\nD.12\nInduction Lemma for Exact Function\nThe goal of this section is to prove Lemma D.29\nLemma D.29 (Induction Lemma). For a fixed i, suppose the following conditions hold\n• Set Condition. Si ⊆[i −1]\n• Budget Condition. |Si| ≤k\n• Value Condition. fSi−1,i−1(Si) ≥(1 −1/e) · (1 −θ)i(1 −γ)iopti\n• Universal Dynamic Condition 1 (for exact function). (See Definition D.25) fSi,i(Si) ≥(1 −θ) ·\nfSi−1,i−1(Si)\n• Universal Dynamic Condition 2. (See Definition D.28) opti ≥(1 −γ) · opti+1\n• Universal Monotone Condition (for exact function). (See Definition D.23) fSi,i(X) ≥fSi,i(Y )\nfor all Y ⊂X\nThen if we construct Si+1 as Definition D.20, then we have\n• Set Condition. Si+1 ⊆[i]\n• Budget Condition. |Si+1| ≤k\n• Value Condition. fSi,i(Si+1) ≥(1 −1/e) · (1 −θ)i+1(1 −γ)i+1opti+1\nProof. Proof of Set Condition.\nNote that Si ⊆[i −1], by using the way we construct Si+1, then it is obvious that Si+1 ⊆[i].\nProof of Budget Condition.\nNote that |Si| ≤k, by using the way we construct Si+1, then it is straightforward that |Si+1| ≤k.\nProof of Value Condition.\nWe can show that\nfSi,i(Si+1) ≥fSi,i(Si)\n≥(1 −θ) · fSi−1,i−1(Si)\n≥(1 −θ) · ((1 −1/e)(1 −θ)i(1 −γ)iopti −i · ϵ0)\n≥(1 −θ) · ((1 −1/e)(1 −θ)i(1 −γ)i+1opti+1)\n≥(1 −1/e) · (1 −θ)i+1(1 −γ)i+1opti+1.\nwhere the first step follows from Universal Monotone Condition, the second step follows from Universal\nDynamic Condition 1, the third step follows from Value Condition, the forth step follows from Universal\nDynamic Condition 2, and the last step follows from simple algebra.\nThus, we complete the proof.\n37\n\n\n=== PDF page 38 ===\nD.13\nInduction Lemma for Approximate Function\nThe goal of this section is to prove Lemma D.30\nLemma D.30 (Induction Lemma). For a fixed i, suppose the following conditions hold\n• Set Condition. Si ⊆[i −1]\n• Budget Condition. |Si| ≤k\n• Value Condition. fSi−1,i−1(Si) ≥(1 −1/e) · (1 −θ)i(1 −γ)iopti −i · ϵ0\n• Universal Dynamic Condition 1 (for approximate function). (see Definition D.26) efSi,i(Si) ≥\n(1 −θ) · efSi−1,i−1(Si)\n• Universal Dynamic Condition 2. opti ≥(1 −γ) · opti+1\n• Universal Approximate Condition. (See Definition D.28) fSi,i(X) ≥efSi,i(X) −ϵ0 for all X\n• Universal Monotone Condition (for approximate function). (see Definition D.24) efSi,i(X) ≥\nefSi,i(Y ) for all Y ⊂X\nThen if we construct Si+1 as Definition D.22, then we have\n• Set Condition. Si+1 ⊆[i]\n• Budget Condition. |Si+1| ≤k\n• Value Condition. fSi,i(Si+1) ≥(1 −1/e) · (1 −θ)i+1(1 −γ)i+1opti+1 −(i + 1) · ϵ0\nProof. Proof of Set Condition.\nNote that Si ⊆[i −1], by using the way we construct Si+1, then it is obvious that Si+1 ⊆[i].\nProof of Budget Condition.\nNote that |Si| ≤k, by using the way we construct Si+1, then it is straightforward that |Si+1| ≤k.\nProof of Value Condition.\nWe can show that\nfSi,i(Si+1) ≥efSi,i(Si+1) −ϵ0\n≥efSi,i(Si) −ϵ0\n≥(1 −θ) · efSi−1,i−1(Si) −ϵ0\n≥(1 −θ) · ((1 −1/e)(1 −θ)i(1 −γ)iopti −i · ϵ0) −ϵ0\n≥(1 −θ) · ((1 −1/e)(1 −θ)i(1 −γ)i+1opti+1 −i · ϵ0) −ϵ0\n≥(1 −1/e) · (1 −θ)i+1(1 −γ)i+1opti+1 −(i + 1) · ϵ0.\nwhere the first step follows from Universal Approximate Condition, the second step follows from\nUniversal Monotone Condition, the third step follows from Universal Dynamic Condition 1, the\nforth step follows from Value Condition, the fifth step follows from Universal Dynamic Condition 2,\nand the last step follows from simple algebra.\nThus, we complete the proof.\nD.14\nTheoretical Result\nWe first give the guarantee of our full-knowledge version (without cache size limitation).\nLemma D.31 (Formal version of Lemma 3.1). Under the mild assumption, let k denote any target size. If\nwe greedily compute the attention score based on full information, then we can find the set Si such that\nf(Si) ≥(1 −1/e) · (1 −α)opti,\nwhere α ∈(0, 1) are parameters.\n38\n\n\n=== PDF page 39 ===\nProof. The proof follows from using Theorem D.17, Corollary D.18, Lemma D.29 with choosing θ = γ =\nα/(10n).\nNext, we show the guarantee for our robust and approximate greedy eviction policy algorithm (Algorithm\n2).\nTheorem D.32 (Formal version of Theorem 4.4). Under the mild assumption, let k denote the budget of\nspace limitation. If for each token, we greedily compute the attention score based on top-k choice, then we can\nshow the set eSi we generate each for token i ∈[n] satisfy that\nf(eSi) ≥(1 −1/e) · (1 −α)opti −β,\nwhere α ∈(0, 1), β > 0 are parameters.\nProof. The proof follows from using Theorem D.17, Corollary D.18, Lemma D.30 with choosing ϵ0 = β/(10n)\nand θ = γ = α/(10n).\nD.15\nExtended Related Work for Theoretical Attention Problems\nThe static attention computation is asking the following question that given Q, K, V ∈Rn×d, the goal\nis to D−1 exp(QK⊤)V where D = diag(exp(QK⊤)1n).\n[82] studied the static attention computation\nfrom both algorithm and hardness.\nOn the positive, they provide an almost linear time algorithm to\napproximately compute the attention matrix. On the negative side, assuming a strong exponential time\nhypothesis (SETH), they prove a hardness result. Their hardness result is, unless SETH fails, there is no\nalgorithm that runs in truly subquadratic time to approximately compute the attention matrix. Further,\n[83] considers the dynamic of attention computation problem. They also provide both algorithmic results\nand hardness results. In the work of [85], they consider the sparsification of attention matrix construction.\nIn particular, they assume that situation that d ≫n, and show how to sparsify the columns of matrix Q.\n[85] provides two algorithms, one is a randomized algorithm, and the other is a deterministic algorithm.\nDifferential privacy is a famous and textbook topic in graduate school, recently the work of [84] shows\nhow to give a differentially private algorithm for computing the attention matrix. For a given A ∈Rn×d\nand vector b ∈Rn, [86] formulates and studies exponential regression minx ∥exp(Ax) −b∥2. Then [87]\nconsiders the normalization factor in exponential regression and defines the softmax regression problem\nminx ∥⟨exp(Ax), 1n⟩−1 exp(Ax) −b∥2. [88] moves the scaling factor from exp(Ax) to b and defines a rescaled\nsoftmax regression problem minx ∥exp(Ax) −⟨exp(Ax), 1n⟩· b∥2.\nD.16\nSparsity Preserving\nRecall that in Figure 2, we observe that even when trained densely, the attention matrices of LLMs are over\n95% sparse at inference time. Only 5% of the KV cache is sufficient for decoding the same output token at\neach generation step. Here, we provide some formal formulations for sparsity.\nDefinition D.33. Suppose the following conditions\n• Let S0 ⊂[m].\n• Let k = |S0|.\n• Let τ ∈(0, 1) denote a threshold for truncating the value.\n• Let α ∈(0, 1) denote a fraction of mass (larger than τ) outside S0.\n• Let mapping D : Rd →Rm\n≥0.\n• For each x ∈Rd, D(x) ∈Rm is a vector that has length m.\nWe say the distribution D is (α, τ, k)-good if the following conditions hold\n• For all x ∈Rd, S0 ⊂suppτ(D(x))\n39\n\n\n=== PDF page 40 ===\n• For all x ∈Rd, | suppτ(D(x))\\S0| ≤α · k\nClaim D.34. Suppose we sample n points {x1, x2, · · · , xn} ⊂Rd from (α, τ, k)-good distribution uniformly\nat random, then we have\n• S0 ⊆∩i∈[n] suppτ(xi)\n• |(∪i∈[n] suppτ(D(x)))\\S0| ≤αkn\nProof. Since for all i ∈[n], we have S0 ⊆suppτ(D(xi)), thus\nS0 ⊆∩i∈[n] suppτ(xi).\nTherefore we proved the first property.\nWe know that for all i ∈[n], we have | suppτ(D(x))\\S0| ≤αkn. Thus\n|(∪i∈[n] suppτ(D(xi)))\\S0| ≤\nn\nX\ni=1\n| suppτ(D(xi)))\\S0| ≤n · αk\nTherefore, we finish the proof for the second property.\nD.17\nDefinition of Loss Function\nIn this section, we follow the theoretical softmax regression literature [87] and define a number of functions\nto make the calculations of gradient and Hessian convenient. If the following conditions hold\n• Let H(x) = A⊤(B(x) + W 2)A denote the Hessian of L.\nThen, we have\n• ∥H(x) −H(y)∥≤n2 exp(40R2)∥x −y∥2\nProof. Given matrix A ∈Rn×d, b ∈Rn, and w ∈Rn.\n• We use x∗to denote the optimal solution of\nmin\nx∈Rd Lexp + Lsparse + Lreg\nthat\n– g(x∗) = 0d, where g denotes the gradient function.\n– ∥x∗∥2 ≤R.\n44\n\n\n=== PDF page 45 ===\n• Suppose that R ≥10, M = exp(Θ(R2 + log n)), and l > 0.\n• Assume that ∥A∥≤R.
